## Round 2 Verdict

Mostly the original review holds, but several claims need narrowing from “this is broken” to “this is the sharpest tension,” and a few UI-weight critiques have to be demoted to “plausible, not proven” given the no-browser limitation. The single clear factual error (two-decimal GPA display) is accepted and removed. The strongest findings remain composite caveats, Defence/Trade mixed construct + tripwire coherence, Flagship probation, inspectability, and single-editor risk. ## Claim-by-Claim Response

### 1. Composite headline critique

**Original claim**: “The Composite Headline Is Still the Dashboard’s Least Defensible Surface.”

**Status**: `Stand by it, but narrow it`

**Response to adjudication**: Your adjudication reframes this as “directionally right, but ‘least defensible’ is too absolute; the core issue is under-exposed caveats.” That’s fair. The composites are central to the product thesis and unusually transparent once a reader opens the derivation panel. The defensibility problem is not that the math is incorrect — it’s that the sensitivity to weighting and the ordinal-to-interval shortcut are not obvious at the point of first contact. **What would count as a sufficient exit rule**:

A concrete rule along these lines:

> After one full cycle, Flagship Delivery remains as a graded dimension only if, in at least one cycle, its combination-rule grade differs by at least one full letter from (a) the simple mean of the five home dimensions, and (b) the pocketbook-weighted composite, and that difference is judged useful by the editor in judgmentDetail. ### 4. Governance heaviness

**Original claim**: “The governance stack has become self-referentially heavy; governance mass is outpacing product clarity and maintenance realism.”

**Status**: `Stand by it, but narrow it`

These meta docs document how to run audits and multi-model reviews, but they also reference each other in loops and need maintenance separate from the actual scoring method. They are editorially useful; they do not increase reader trust directly; they’re costly for a successor editor to grok. So I narrow: the governance stack is not “mostly drag,” but **there is a meta-governance sub-stack that could reasonably be collapsed into a single “how verification works here” doc.**

**What still matters now**:
- Core scoring governance is a strength; meta-governance is where simplification is warranted. ### 5. False precision / score display

**Original claim**: “Composite GPA displayed to two decimal places adds false precision; change to one decimal and disclaim ordinal→interval shortcut.”

**Status**: `Stand by it, but narrow it`

**Response to adjudication**: You correctly point out that the live code already uses `.toFixed(1)` in `Dashboard.jsx`. My “two decimals like 1.65 vs 1.45” claim is stale and wrong. I accept that. - The **underlying measurement model** still maps letter grades to continuous GPA points and averages them. That’s an ordinal→interval shortcut and is acknowledged in v2 docs. - They are **design memory**; they’re not active feature code.
- Maintaining them in lockstep with v1 governance is optional. The drag comes not from existence but from continuous maintenance.

So I narrow:

> Full v2 performance layer should remain parked; v2 docs should stop growing and only be touched when v1 changes truly invalidate a specific design assumption. They don’t need to be purged, but they should be treated as archival design artefacts rather than a live roadmap.

**What still matters now**:
- Adding tri-lens to 11 dimensions is a big complexity jump; doing so before v1 stabilizes is risky. ## Best Next Move

If I had to recommend only one next move after this back-and-forth, it would be:

**Implement the Affordability Response Inspectability Pilot (“Why not higher / Why not lower”) as a v1 drawer enhancement.**
